What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… the main thing is I keep running into this wall with work that takes real concentration. Like, I manage health services stuff, and when I have to sit down and do monthly reports or plan out a multi-site health event, I know it matters, but I’ll put it off and put it off.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t care—actually sometimes I care too much, if that makes sense. I’ll open the file, then suddenly I’m checking something else, or thinking about who needs what room, or remembering some issue from last year’s event… anyway, the simple version is: any heavy “sit down and think” task feels almost impossible to start, and that’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you think about starting these heavy 'sit down and think' tasks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it anxious exactly. Maybe sometimes, but it’s hard to separate out. I’ll think, “Okay, I need to start this report,” and then my brain kind of… goes everywhere else.\n\nLike last week I sat down to do one and ended up thinking about whether we had enough staff coverage for a totally different clinic day, and then I remembered I hadn’t replied to someone about parking passes, which—honestly, the parking situation at our building is its own mess—but yeah, I’m not sure if that’s anxiety or just me getting pulled off track.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worried or on edge when you're not working on these tasks?\n\nPatient: Um… when I’m not working on them? I don’t think I’m generally on edge, no. Like, I can be bothered because I know the thing is still sitting there, but I wouldn’t say I’m walking around worried all the time.\n\nSometimes I’ll remember it at a weird moment—like driving home or standing in line for coffee—and think, “Oh right, that report,” and then my mind jumps to something else, like whether I returned a call or what we’re doing for dinner. So… I’m not sure. I don’t really notice feeling worried unless I’m actively trying to deal with the task, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any habits or rituals that you feel compelled to do over and over?\n\nPatient: Um… habits or rituals? I’m not sure. Like, I have routines at work because if I don’t, things kind of turn into a pile—forms, emails, sign-in sheets, that kind of thing.\n\nBut “compelled,” like I have to do something over and over or something bad will happen? I don’t think so. I mean, I’ll re-check an email sometimes before sending it, but then again half the time I’m checking because I forgot whether I attached the file, and then I notice the subject line could be clearer, and then—sorry. No, I don’t think I have rituals like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things in general, even when there's no obvious reason to worry?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a general way. I mean, there are things I think about, sure—work stuff, bills, whether I remembered to do something—but I wouldn’t say I’m just worrying for no reason.\n\nSometimes my mind is busy, but it’s more like it jumps around. Like I’ll think about one thing and then suddenly I’m thinking about the copier being out of toner or whether I need to stop at the store. But I don’t know if I’d call that worry, exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find your attention being pulled away by unrelated things when you're trying to focus?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that happens a lot. Like in meetings, I’ll be trying to listen to a budget update, and then someone’s phone vibrates on the table, or people start talking out in the hallway, and I’m just… gone for a minute.\n\nOr not even something outside—sometimes it’s just a thought, like, “Oh, I forgot to send that thing for the league,” and then I’m thinking about that, and by the time I tune back in I’ve missed the next few minutes of what they were saying. It’s frustrating, because I was trying to pay attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things even when there's nothing specific happening that would cause worry?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that. Like… if there’s nothing specific going on, I don’t usually sit there feeling worried out of nowhere.\n\nI do sometimes have this kind of background mental noise, but it’s more like thinking about stuff I may have forgotten, or jumping from one thing to another. Like I’ll remember something about work, then think about an errand, then wonder if I ever returned a call—actually, I need to check that later—but no, I wouldn’t call it worry when nothing’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to stick with one task for a long time without getting distracted?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I can start out trying to stay with something, like in a meeting when they’re going over the budget, and then something small will pull me off track. Somebody’s phone buzzes on the table, or people are talking out in the hallway, and suddenly I’m listening to that instead.\n\nSometimes it’s not even outside noise—it’ll be a thought, like, “Oh, I forgot to do that thing for the league,” and then I’m off thinking about that. And then I realize I missed the next few minutes of what was being said. It’s annoying because I’m not trying to drift off, it just sort of happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel fidgety or have trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think of myself as, like, bouncing out of my chair or anything. I mean, I’ll shift around sometimes, but I think most people do that, right?\n\nAt work I’m usually sitting in meetings, and—actually, our conference room chairs are terrible, so maybe that’s part of it—but I haven’t really noticed a big problem with needing to get up all the time. I guess I’d say not especially, unless you mean something more specific.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your time and prioritizing tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that one’s definitely a problem. I’ll have work projects going, and then stuff for the memorial committee, and then outreach events, and I’ll think I’m keeping track of it all, but I’ve got notes in three different places.\n\nLike one list is in my notebook, then something’s in my phone, then there’s an email I meant to flag—actually, I still need to clean out my inbox, it’s ridiculous—but anyway, I have trouble figuring out what has to happen first. So I’ll spend time on something that maybe isn’t urgent, and then suddenly the important thing is due and I’m rushing at the end. It’s frustrating because I’m busy the whole time, but not always on the right thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about everyday things, like finances or relationships, even when things are going well?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, everybody worries about money and relationships sometimes, right? Like if there’s a big bill or something going on with family, yeah, I’ll think about it.\n\nBut even when things are going well… I don’t know that I’d say I’m sitting around worrying all the time. Sometimes my mind jumps to random stuff, like whether I replied to someone or if I forgot some errand, and then that makes me think of other things. Actually, my wife jokes that I can turn one thought into ten, but I don’t know if that’s worry exactly.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed it as a big everyday worry thing, but I may not be separating it right.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself preoccupied with concerns about everyday things, like money or relationships, even when there's no immediate problem?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it preoccupied. I mean, money comes up, sure—bills, insurance stuff, all that—and relationships, I guess if there’s been a tense conversation or something. But if there’s no actual problem happening, I don’t think I’m sitting there worrying about it nonstop.\n\nAlthough sometimes I’ll think, “Did I forget to pay something?” and then that reminds me of the mail pile on the counter, which reminds me I was supposed to buy stamps—do people even use stamps much anymore? My mom still sends cards for everything.\n\nSorry, what was the question again? If it’s whether I’m worried all the time for no reason, I don’t think so. Not that I’ve really noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things for a long time after they've been resolved?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I do that, at least not in a big way. If something is really resolved, I usually kind of move on… though sometimes I’ll remember it later and think, “Did I handle that right?” or “Was there something else I was supposed to do?”\n\nBut I don’t think I sit with it for a long time, like replaying it for days. Unless—well, I guess if it involved another person, I might think about the conversation again, like if I sounded short or forgot to say something. That reminds me, I still need to call my brother back about this family thing, but that’s not really what you asked.\n\nSo, no, I wouldn’t say I often keep worrying after something’s been resolved. Not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about what might happen in the future?\n\nPatient: I mean… sometimes, I guess? Like I’ll think about what’s coming up at work or whether something’s going to fall through, but I don’t know if I’d say I often worry about the future in that big-picture way.\n\nIt’s more like I’ll jump ahead—“What if I forgot something for next week?” or “Do I need to handle that appointment?”—and then I’m thinking about three other things, like my calendar or something I meant to write down. I actually bought this planner once, and I used it for maybe two weeks… anyway.\n\nSo, I’m not sure. I don’t feel like I’m constantly worried about what might happen, but I do think ahead in bursts, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about everyday things, like work, finances, or relationships, even when there's nothing specific to worry about?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m not sure I’d say I worry when there’s nothing specific. I don’t feel like I’m constantly worried about what might happen, but I do think ahead in bursts, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about everyday things, like work, finances, or relationships, even when there's nothing specific to worry about?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m not sure I’d say I worry when there’s nothing specific. Like work, finances, relationships—those things are always kind of there in the background, but I usually need something to trigger it, like an email I forgot to answer or a bill sitting on the counter.\n\nThen again, sometimes I’ll start thinking, “Wait, did I miss something?” and then I’m checking my phone, and then I remember I was supposed to look up something totally different. Actually, the other day I found an old receipt in my jacket and spent ten minutes trying to figure out why I saved it… which probably isn’t what you’re asking.\n\nSo, no, I don’t think I’m just worrying about everyday stuff for no reason all the time. I don’t know if I’d say my mind goes completely blank, but I’ll lose the thread of what I’m supposed to be focusing on.\n\nLike in meetings, I’ll be trying to listen to a budget update, and then somebody’s phone vibrates, or people are talking out in the hallway, and suddenly I’m thinking about that instead. I mean, I can get irritated, like anyone, especially if something’s been dragging on at work or there’s confusion about who was supposed to handle what.\n\nBut calming down after? Hmm. I’m not sure. I mean, if I’m not doing anything, I usually end up doing something pretty quickly — checking my phone, looking at a note, getting up to straighten something.

But whether that’s because I can’t quiet my mind or just because I’m used to being busy… I don’t know. Relaxing is kind of a vague word for me. Like, I can sit and watch TV, but then sometimes I’m also thinking about whether I paid a bill or what time I need to leave tomorrow, or I’ll look something up and then I’m not really watching anymore.

So… maybe a little? I wouldn’t say I’ve really tracked it. I don’t always notice until someone points out that I’m not actually relaxing.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… the main thing is I keep running into this wall with work that takes real concentration. Like, I manage health services stuff, and when I have to sit down and do monthly reports or plan out a multi-site health event, I know it matters, but I’ll put it off and put it off.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t care—actually sometimes I care too much, if that makes sense. I’ll open the file, then suddenly I’m checking something else, or thinking about who needs what room, or remembering some issue from last year’s event… anyway, the simple version is: any heavy “sit down and think” task feels almost impossible to start, and that’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you think about starting these heavy 'sit down and think' tasks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it anxious exactly. Maybe sometimes, but it’s hard to separate out. I’ll think, “Okay, I need to start this report,” and then my brain kind of… goes everywhere else.\n\nLike last week I sat down to do one and ended up thinking about whether we had enough staff coverage for a totally different clinic day, and then I remembered I hadn’t replied to someone about parking passes, which—honestly, the parking situation at our building is its own mess—but yeah, I’m not sure if that’s anxiety or just me getting pulled off track.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worried or on edge when you're not working on these tasks?\n\nPatient: Um… when I’m not working on them? I don’t think I’m generally on edge, no. It’s frustrating, because I was trying to pay attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things even when there's nothing specific happening that would cause worry?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that. Like I’ll remember something about work, then think about an errand, then wonder if I ever returned a call—actually, I need to check that later—but no, I wouldn’t call it worry when nothing’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to stick with one task for a long time without getting distracted?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I can start out trying to stay with something, like in a meeting when they’re going over the budget, and then something small will pull me off track. Somebody’s phone buzzes on the table, or people are talking out in the hallway, and suddenly I’m listening to that instead.\n\nSometimes it’s not even outside noise—it’ll be a thought, like, “Oh, I forgot to do that thing for the league,” and then I’m off thinking about that. And then I realize I missed the next few minutes of what was being said. It’s annoying because I’m not trying to drift off, it just sort of happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel fidgety or have trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think of myself as, like, bouncing out of my chair or anything. I mean, I’ll shift around sometimes, but I think most people do that, right?\n\nAt work I’m usually sitting in meetings, and—actually, our conference room chairs are terrible, so maybe that’s part of it—but I haven’t really noticed a big problem with needing to get up all the time. I guess I’d say not especially, unless you mean something more specific.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your time and prioritizing tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that one’s definitely a problem. I’ll have work projects going, and then stuff for the memorial committee, and then outreach events, and I’ll think I’m keeping track of it all, but I’ve got notes in three different places.\n\nLike one list is in my notebook, then something’s in my phone, then there’s an email I meant to flag—actually, I still need to clean out my inbox, it’s ridiculous—but anyway, I have trouble figuring out what has to happen first. So I’ll spend time on something that maybe isn’t urgent, and then suddenly the important thing is due and I’m rushing at the end. It’s frustrating because I’m busy the whole time, but not always on the right thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about everyday things, like finances or relationships, even when things are going well?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, everybody worries about money and relationships sometimes, right? Like if there’s a big bill or something going on with family, yeah, I’ll think about it.\n\nBut even when things are going well… I don’t know that I’d say I’m sitting around worrying all the time. Sometimes my mind jumps to random stuff, like whether I replied to someone or if I forgot some errand, and then that makes me think of other things. Actually, my wife jokes that I can turn one thought into ten, but I don’t know if that’s worry exactly.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed it as a big everyday worry thing, but I may not be separating it right.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself preoccupied with concerns about everyday things, like money or relationships, even when there's no immediate problem?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it preoccupied. I mean, money comes up, sure—bills, insurance stuff, all that—and relationships, I guess if there’s been a tense conversation or something. But if there’s no actual problem happening, I don’t think I’m sitting there worrying about it nonstop.\n\nAlthough sometimes I’ll think, “Did I forget to pay something?” and then that reminds me of the mail pile on the counter, which reminds me I was supposed to buy stamps—do people even use stamps much anymore? Not that I’ve really noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things for a long time after they've been resolved?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I do that, at least not in a big way. If something is really resolved, I usually kind of move on… though sometimes I’ll remember it later and think, “Did I handle that right?” or “Was there something else I was supposed to do?”\n\nBut I don’t think I sit with it for a long time, like replaying it for days. Unless—well, I guess if it involved another person, I might think about the conversation again, like if I sounded short or forgot to say something. That reminds me, I still need to call my brother back about this family thing, but that’s not really what you asked.\n\nSo, no, I wouldn’t say I often keep worrying after something’s been resolved. Not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about what might happen in the future?\n\nPatient: I mean… sometimes, I guess? Like I’ll think about what’s coming up at work or whether something’s going to fall through, but I don’t know if I’d say I often worry about the future in that big-picture way.\n\nIt’s more like I’ll jump ahead—“What if I forgot something for next week?” or “Do I need to handle that appointment?”—and then I’m thinking about three other things, like my calendar or something I meant to write down. I actually bought this planner once, and I used it for maybe two weeks… anyway.\n\nSo, I’m not sure. I don’t feel like I’m constantly worried about what might happen, but I do think ahead in bursts, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about everyday things, like work, finances, or relationships, even when there's nothing specific to worry about?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m not sure I’d say I worry when there’s nothing specific. Like work, finances, relationships—those things are always kind of there in the background, but I usually need something to trigger it, like an email I forgot to answer or a bill sitting on the counter.\n\nThen again, sometimes I’ll start thinking, “Wait, did I miss something?” and then I’m checking my phone, and then I remember I was supposed to look up something totally different. Actually, the other day I found an old receipt in my jacket and spent ten minutes trying to figure out why I saved it… which probably isn’t what you’re asking.\n\nSo, no, I don’t think I’m just worrying about everyday stuff for no reason all the time. At least I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your mind goes blank or you have trouble remembering things when you're trying to focus on a task?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that happens. I don’t know if I’d say my mind goes completely blank, but I’ll lose the thread of what I’m supposed to be focusing on.\n\nLike in meetings, I’ll be trying to listen to a budget update, and then somebody’s phone vibrates, or people are talking out in the hallway, and suddenly I’m thinking about that instead. Or I’ll remember something I forgot to do for the league—sorry, I help with a league, and there’s always some little thing—and then I realize I missed the next few minutes of what was being said.\n\nThen I’m sitting there trying to piece it back together from whatever slide they’re on, and I’m like, “Wait, how did we get here?” So yeah, it’s more like I get pulled off track and then I don’t remember what I just heard.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble calming down after you get upset or angry?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I’m not sure I’d describe that as a big issue for me. I mean, I can get irritated, like anyone, especially if something’s been dragging on at work or there’s confusion about who was supposed to handle what.\n\nBut calming down after? Usually I think I’m okay. I might be annoyed for a bit, and then something else needs my attention and I move on. Although sometimes I’ll replay a conversation later, like, “Did I come across too harsh?” But that’s more after the fact.\n\nSo… I don’t think I often have trouble calming down, no. Not that I’ve really noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things for no apparent reason?\n\nPatient: Not really for no apparent reason, no. I mean, I can get caught up thinking about something, but usually there’s at least some reason for it, even if it’s small.\n\nLike, if I see a message I forgot to answer, then I’ll think about that, and then I might remember three other things I was supposed to handle. But that’s not the same as just sitting there worrying out of nowhere, I don’t think.\n\nSometimes I’m not great at telling whether there was a trigger, though. Like I’ll be halfway through a thought and realize I don’t remember how I got there. But overall, no, I wouldn’t say I often worry for no reason.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things even when there's no obvious reason to worry and it's hard to control?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m not totally sure how to separate that from just normal thinking things over. Like if there’s no obvious reason, then usually I don’t think I’m worrying — but sometimes I’ll be mentally chewing on something and then realize I don’t even know what started it.\n\nBut “hard to control,” I don’t know. I can usually shift if I have to, especially if someone comes in or there’s something concrete to do. Although then I might get sidetracked by that, too. Like yesterday I went to answer one email and ended up looking at a scheduling issue for somebody else, which is not really worry, I guess.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say that happens often. Or at least I haven’t noticed it as a big thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to quiet your mind and relax when you're not actively doing something?\n\nPatient: Hmm. I’m not sure. Although sometimes I’ll replay a conversation later, like, “Did I come across too harsh?” But that’s more after the fact.\n\nSo… I don’t think I often have trouble calming down, no. Not that I’ve really noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things for no apparent reason?\n\nPatient: Not really for no apparent reason, no. I mean, I can get caught up thinking about something, but usually there’s at least some reason for it, even if it’s small.\n\nLike, if I see a message I forgot to answer, then I’ll think about that, and then I might remember three other things I was supposed to handle. But that’s not the same as just sitting there worrying out of nowhere, I don’t think.\n\nSometimes I’m not great at telling whether there was a trigger, though. Like I’ll be halfway through a thought and realize I don’t remember how I got there. But overall, no, I wouldn’t say I often worry for no reason.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things even when there's no obvious reason to worry and it's hard to control?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m not totally sure how to separate that from just normal thinking things over. Like if there’s no obvious reason, then usually I don’t think I’m worrying — but sometimes I’ll be mentally chewing on something and then realize I don’t even know what started it.\n\nBut “hard to control,” I don’t know. I can usually shift if I have to, especially if someone comes in or there’s something concrete to do. Although then I might get sidetracked by that, too. Like yesterday I went to answer one email and ended up looking at a scheduling issue for somebody else, which is not really worry, I guess.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say that happens often. Or at least I haven’t noticed it as a big thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to quiet your mind and relax when you're not actively doing something?\n\nPatient: Hmm. I’m not sure. I mean, if I’m not doing anything, I usually end up doing something pretty quickly — checking my phone, looking at a note, getting up to straighten something.\n\nBut whether that’s because I can’t quiet my mind or just because I’m used to being busy… I don’t know. Relaxing is kind of a vague word for me. Like, I can sit and watch TV, but then sometimes I’m also thinking about whether I paid a bill or what time I need to leave tomorrow, or I’ll look something up and then I’m not really watching anymore.\n\nSo… maybe a little? I wouldn’t say I’ve really tracked it. I don’t always notice until someone points out that I’m not actually relaxing."
